### Post #199: 2025-10-26 13:22 — `$HUMA` `[Dd]`

**$HUMA Series Post 6: Insider Transactions**

Between mid-August and early September, a cluster of Form 4s hit showing more than two million shares offloaded in days. On the surface, it looked bad, the biggest sale since the company went public, and it came shortly after Humacyte’s first FDA approval.

The seller was Brady W. Dougan, former Credit Suisse CEO, long-time Humacyte backer, and husband of founder-CEO Dr. Laura Niklason. Through his investment vehicle Ayabudge LLC, Dougan sold roughly 2.24 million shares over three sessions at prices ranging from $1.83 down to $1.58, effectively taking his position to zero.

That headline alone set off a wave of retail outrage, the CEO’s husband bailing at the lows?  But the truth, buried in the footnotes, told a very different story.

According to filings stretching back to 2023, Ayabudge had pledged about 8.2 million shares as collateral in a private financing arrangement. In plain English, a margin loan.  When Humacyte’s stock slipped below $2 in mid-2025, the collateral cushion vanished.  The lender wanted repayment.  Rather than face a forced liquidation, Dougan arranged a sale to pay down leverage.

The Form 4 even says it outright: “The shares were sold by Ayabudge LLC to allow Mr. Dougan to pay down leverage and to provide a mechanism for investors to acquire shares of the Issuer…”

That second clause, “provide a mechanism for investors,” is legal shorthand for a privately negotiated block trade, not a random market dump.  It likely means Dougan sold shares directly to interested institutional buyers at market-adjacent prices.  It was a structured unwind, not panic.  

Meanwhile, Dr. Niklason never sold a share.  Her trust still holds millions, and nothing in management’s behavior or filings hinted at wavering confidence.  The science kept moving forward. Dialysis durability studies, Durham manufacturing expansion, and preparation for the 2026 BLA filing.

The optics are brutal: a household insider unloading millions of shares just after approval. The reality was mechanical: debt maintenance and bad timing. Margin calls rarely, if ever, happen at the right time. Insider selling makes for an easy headline, but looking below the surface here paints a different story. Reminding us that even the rich can get margin-called.

### Post #200: 2025-10-27 13:58 — `$HUMA` `[Dd]`

**$HUMA Series Post 7: The Trials**

The V005 trial, run under the Department of Defense, put SYMVESS into battlefield chaos: shredded arteries, contaminated wounds, open fractures. Places synthetics can’t survive.

The results were definitively positive.
30-day secondary patency: 90.2%.
Primary patency: 84.3%.
Infection rate: 2.0%.
Ruptures: none.
Device-related amputations: none.

Every loss traced back to injury severity, not graft failure. In expanded-access data (V017), it got even stronger. 91.5% patency with infection below 1% all in live-fire trauma cases.

Then came V007, the hemodialysis access trial. This time, the enemy wasn’t contamination or blood loss, but time.

242 high-risk patients.
Functional patency: 85.7% at 6 months vs 51.9% for fistulas.
Secondary patency: 76.8% at 12 months vs 46.3%.
Infection rates: a fraction of synthetics.
Maturation: immediate use, no waiting period.

The trial has already delivered its top-line data with endpoints met and superiority shown, but formal follow-up continues through 24 months ahead of the supplemental Biologics License Application (BLA) expected in the second half of 2026. The scientific hurdle is cleared; what’s left is durability tracking and manufacturing validation.

Same vessel. Different war. V005 trials proved it could save a limb. V007 trials are proving it can sustain a life.

### Post #201: 2025-10-28 14:25 — `$HUMA` `[Dd]`

**$HUMA Series Post 8: Regulatory & Reimbursement Roadmap**

The science is settled. A lab-grown human vessel can save a limb and restore blood flow. Now comes the slower part, translating that proof into permanent approvals, global access, and reliable payment.

$HUMA crossed its first major line last year, when the FDA approved SYMVESS for use in traumatic vascular injuries, the first human acellular vessel ever cleared for implantation. It wasn’t just a win for one product; it created a legal and scientific foundation that every future application will build on. Rather than starting from scratch, each new indication, like dialysis access, is reviewed as an extension of that same license. It’s the difference between writing a new rulebook and adding a new chapter.

The next chapter is dialysis. The pivotal V007 clinical trial has completed enrollment and met its goals, showing that Humacyte’s vessel lasts longer, resists infection, and can be used immediately, outperforming both synthetic grafts and surgeon-made fistulas. The company is now gathering long-term durability data, typically required for biologic products, before filing for regulatory approval in 2026. Because the vessel and manufacturing process are identical to what’s already approved, this next review should be faster and less risky.

Outside the United States, Humacyte is already laying groundwork for international access:
	•	In Europe, regulators have accepted Humacyte’s clinical data and are guiding the company toward a marketing submission soon after the U.S. filing.
	•	In the United Kingdom, it’s under review through a new fast-track pathway designed for regenerative medicine.
	•	In Japan, early collaboration is underway through their regenerative health program, where domestic partners often accelerate approval timelines.

Regulatory approval, however, is only half of the commercial equation. The other half is reimbursement, getting hospitals and payers to cover the cost.

Humacyte’s initial approval qualifies it for a temporary “new technology” payment program that helps hospitals adopt innovations that reduce complications and readmissions. But the real goal is permanent coding and coverage, which allows hospitals to use SYMVESS without seeking special exceptions. That process is already in motion.

Behind the scenes, the company is supplying cost-effectiveness data to private insurers and Medicare, showing that fewer infections, surgeries, and amputations translate to lower total cost per patient, even at a $24,000 price point. It’s the kind of math that health systems actually care about: save one major infection, and the graft has already paid for itself.

Outside the U.S., the economics are even clearer. In single-payer systems, like most of Europe and military healthcare, the government pays directly for outcomes. Longer-lasting grafts mean fewer hospital visits and surgeries, which immediately reduces national costs. In those systems, durability isn’t just a clinical advantage; it’s a financial one.

The next twelve months are about finishing the checklist, completing two-year durability data for the dialysis indication, finalizing manufacturing validation at the Durham facility, and securing permanent reimbursement status.

After that, Humacyte will have what almost no regenerative medicine company ever achieves: a repeatable, global regulatory framework and a clear reimbursement pathway that rewards its biology for doing what it does best… lasting.

### Post #203: 2025-10-29 14:04 — `$HUMA` `[Dd]`

**$HUMA Series Post 9: Management & Governance**

When a biotech crosses from the lab into commercialization, the playbook changes. It stops being about discovery and starts being about delivery, manufacturing reliability, pricing strategy, hospital adoption, payer alignment. Most companies stumble there. Humacyte didn’t hire for that phase by accident.

Dr. Laura Niklason, the founder and CEO, is the inventor of the human acellular vessel and a vascular surgeon by training. Her background as both clinician and engineer is what makes Humacyte’s science different: it wasn’t built in pursuit of a paper or patent; it was built to solve a surgical problem she faced herself. That perspective doesn’t just inspire credibility, it anchors execution. Few CEOs can walk into a trauma center, explain their own IND data line by line, and still talk reimbursement economics with hospital admins.

Below her sits a leadership team structured like a med-tech company, not a research lab.
	•	Dale Sander, CFO, spent two decades turning CryoLife (now Artivion) into a profitable cardiovascular platform balancing capital discipline with scale.
	•	Heather Prichard, COO, came out of advanced biologics manufacturing and now oversees Humacyte’s Durham facility, one of the few commercial-scale human tissue plants in the world.
	•	Shamik Parikh, Chief Technology & Scientific Officer, bridges tissue engineering and regulatory affairs, the quiet linchpin between innovation and compliance.

The board mirrors that mix of science, policy, and commercialization:
	•	Kathleen Sebelius, former U.S. Secretary of Health and Human Services, brings unmatched insight into the health-policy ecosystem that ultimately determines payment and access.
	•	John Bamforth, a 30-year Eli Lilly veteran, built and scaled multiple global drug franchises, exactly the kind of go-to-market DNA most early-stage biotechs lack.
	•	Dr. Emery Brown, professor at Harvard and MIT, adds academic gravitas and multidisciplinary oversight, grounding Humacyte’s innovation in scientific rigor even as it commercializes.

Still, the chatter in investor circles with concerns about the CEO is real. Across retail forums and social feeds, you’ll find versions of the same line: “Laura is a brilliant scientist, but not the one to scale it.” Some posts reference her visible frustration during the FDA controversy, arguing it showed a lack of PR polish. Others point to her spouse’s 2025 stock sale as proof of wavering confidence, despite the filing footnote that clarified it was for debt reduction and liquidity maintenance. A few compare her to academic founders who’ve historically handed off the reins once commercialization began, framing it as inevitable rather than necessary.

In short, the criticism isn’t about intellect; it’s about optics. She’s a scientist leading in a field where investors are conditioned to expect a salesperson. But optics aren’t outcomes.

What’s often missed in those threads is that Humacyte built the operational depth that most of those critics are calling for. The founder doesn’t need to be replaced because she’s already surrounded by the people who handle the scale phase. The operating bench has scaled regulated vascular products before. The CFO has run the P&L of a public cardiovascular company. The board includes former policymakers and commercial veterans who’ve negotiated pricing frameworks at the national level.

The pattern is familiar to anyone who studies long-term compounders. The same doubts were leveled at Bezos (“retail guy can’t build logistics”), at Musk (“engineer can’t run a car company”), and at countless others before the market realized that founder conviction paired with professional operators is the most resilient structure in business.

That’s the 100-bagger formula: the visionary who refuses to delegate the core idea, surrounded by operators who make it reproducible. Humacyte fits that template exactly.

### Post #206: 2025-10-30 16:23 — `$HUMA` `[Dd]`

**$HUMA Series Post 10: Manufacturing & Margins**

In biotech, margins collapse when production scales. In med-tech, they expand. $HUMA is built like the latter.

The company’s LUNA200 system, its proprietary manufacturing platform, is what turns tissue engineering from an experiment into an industry. Each LUNA bioreactor grows 200 human vessels at once under identical conditions. There’s no donor dependence and no batch-to-batch variability. It’s the same human graft every time, built to spec.

That standardization changes everything about the cost curve. Once a LUNA line is qualified, labor, QA, and materials barely move while output doubles. Early commercial batches are already trending toward 50–60% gross margins, and as Durham’s full-scale facility ramps, those margins could approach 70%+.

The math is simple:
	•	Fixed cost leverage: the cleanroom, technicians, and compliance overhead are already paid for. Every new graft produced drives down per-unit cost.
	•	Yield consistency: no biological rejects, no donor failures, no lost inventory.
	•	Automated monitoring: AI-based process control reduces validation time and release lag.

The real story isn’t that Humacyte makes tissue; it’s that it makes identical tissue at scale. That’s why the company stopped being a “research biotech” the moment the Durham plant came online.

By 2026, when dialysis approval adds thousands of annual units to the run-rate, the manufacturing economics become self-reinforcing: more volume → higher margins → cheaper capital → faster scale. The same loop that built med-tech giants.

### Post #210: 2025-10-31 14:46 — `$HUMA` `[Dd]`

**$HUMA Series Post 11: SYMVESS Competitive Landscape**

Every vascular graft on the market forces a tradeoff between infection risk, durability, and supply. Humacyte’s SYMVESS is the first to remove that tradeoff entirely.

ePTFE synthetics (Gore-Tex, Fluoropassiv, etc.)
These are the workhorses, cheap, durable, and available on demand. But they’re plastic. They don’t integrate, they don’t heal, and they create perfect surfaces for bacteria to colonize. Once infection sets in, the graft has to be cut out. In trauma, they’re useless; in dialysis, they’re maintenance nightmares.

Allografts (cadaveric vessels)
These are human donor vessels, harvested post-mortem, sterilized, and cryopreserved. They sound ideal, living tissue without synthetic material, but sterilization damages collagen and elastin, the fibers that give arteries strength. That’s why they degenerate faster, lose compliance, and fail within months to a few years. Supply is unpredictable, matching is inconsistent, and disease-screening adds cost and delay. They’re a stopgap, used when infection risk rules out synthetics but autologous vein isn’t available.

Autologous vein (the patient’s own)
Still the textbook “gold standard,” but half of patients don’t have suitable veins due to prior surgeries, diabetes, or vascular disease. Even when available, harvest adds another wound and doubles recovery time. Patency rates drop sharply after a year, and revision surgery is common. What used to be the best option is now just the least bad.

SYMVESS closes all those gaps. It’s made from human cells grown on a biodegradable scaffold, then decellularized to leave a living framework the body can recognize as its own. Once implanted, the patient’s cells migrate into it, rebuilding it as native tissue. It resists infection because it becomes part of the host. It lasts because it remodels instead of wearing down.

In clinical use, SYMVESS has shown:
	•	Infection rates under 2 % (vs. 15–30 % for synthetics)
	•	Patency over 80 % at one year (vs. 40–50 % for autologous vein)
	•	Zero device-related amputations in trauma

The combination of biologic durability, low infection, and virtually unlimited supply  simply hasn’t existed before. Every competitor is fighting biology: synthetics repel it, allografts decay from it, veins exhaust it. And then there’s SYMVESS, not fighting biology, but built with it. 

*Link: https://afterhour.com/YungEmber/8o3/dollarhuma-series-post-11-symv*

### Post #211: 2025-10-31 16:13 `[Dd]`

**You probably still don’t know why Asset Vault is a game changer for $NRGV**

Dilution is the silent killer of many small-cap companies. The story usually starts the same way, a promising technology, early wins, and then a series of equity raises to fund growth. Each raise chips away at ownership, and by the time the business finally scales, original shareholders are left with a fraction of what they started. Capital is needed to fuel growth, but dilution still presents a risk to investor returns.

That’s what makes Asset Vault, Energy Vault’s new platform, so important. It’s a blueprint for how a small-cap company can scale aggressively without issuing a single new share.

Here’s how it works. Each project under Asset Vault sits in its own special purpose vehicle (SPV). Energy Vault provides the storage technology, system integration, and operations expertise, while external partners fund the project through preferred equity, not common shares. That’s the critical shift. The capital enters at the project level, so corporate shareholders aren’t diluted when the company scales.

The anchor investor for the platform is Orion Infrastructure Capital (OIC), a $3 billion infrastructure fund with deep experience financing energy and power assets. OIC has committed $300 million in preferred equity to fund the first wave of Asset Vault projects, including a 150 MW / 300 MWh system in Texas. Their preferred equity carries a fixed, contractual return, paid out of project cash flows during the first eight years, which aligns with the term of the initial offtake agreement. During that period, OIC receives priority distributions until their return is met.

What happens after that is where the model becomes transformative. Once OIC’s preferred equity term ends, the capital + return is effectively repaid through cash flow, and Energy Vault retains 100% ownership of the underlying asset. Every dollar of revenue from the long-term offtake then flows directly to Energy Vault. The company transitions from one-time project sales to recurring, infrastructure-style income turning stored energy into an annuity stream.

Another smart detail is how Energy Vault structured this for scalability. Asset Vault isn’t a one-off joint venture; it’s a replicable framework. Each project SPV can bring in its own funding partner, using the same non-dilutive template OIC established. That means the platform can attract multiple institutional investors to finance additional builds, compounding project-level growth without ever tapping the equity market.

Even better, Energy Vault will self-perform engineering, procurement, and construction (EPC) services for each project. That allows them to earn revenue during the build-out phase, which offsets corporate expenses and development costs long before the project becomes revenue-producing. It’s a way to monetize their internal capabilities upfront while still keeping the long-term yield once operations begin.

In plain terms: OIC’s preferred equity funds the build, the offtake guarantees revenue, Energy Vault earns fees during construction, and when the financing term ends, they own the entire asset outright. No dilution, no repeated capital raises, no dependence on short-term market sentiment.

This structure gives Energy Vault something most small caps never achieve, a path to scale that’s both capital-efficient and self-sustaining.

### Post #213: 2025-11-01 14:55 — `$HUMA` `[Dd]`

**$HUMA Series Post 12: A Broken Chart**

Every investor has lived through one. That painful stretch where everything under the surface is improving and yet the stock trades like it’s circling the drain. 2025 has been that year for $HUMA. 

But the good news for early investors is that the story didn’t fall apart although it did get a little messy. Much of this has already been covered in the series, but this post will provide a concise and consolidated narrative of the 60% share price decline this year. 

Humacyte got its big win in June 2024 when the FDA approved SYMVESS, the first lab-grown human vessel cleared for trauma use. That should’ve been the start of the rerate. Instead, it marked the top.

Almost immediately, critics came out of the woodwork, a few doctors claiming the approval process was rushed, a Reddit echo chamber feeding on it, and even Martin Shkreli weighing in like he suddenly cared about clinical integrity. The science didn’t change, but sentiment certainly did.

By early 2025, reality set in. Hospitals move slowly, and early revenue from trauma grafts barely cracked seven figures. Forums started turning skeptical. A familiar refrain appeared: “Brilliant scientist, but can she scale?” The idea that founder-led science companies can’t make the commercial leap and it’s the oldest fear in biotech.

Then came the optics problem. The CEO’s husband, Brady Dougan, sold shares early in 2025, not a (lack of) confidence play, but a margin call. It was mechanical, but the nuance didn’t matter. On paper, it looked like an insider cashing out right as the stock rolled over.

Around that same time, Humacyte raised a small round, a bridge ahead of the dialysis filing, and then a larger $60 million raise in October to extend the runway through 2026. Both were rational, clean financings. But by the second one, the story had already shifted. To traders, it wasn’t “funding for growth,” it was “more dilution.”

Add it all up, controversy, slow revenue, a forced insider sale, two raises in one year, and growing doubt around whether a scientist can become a CEO who scales and you get the 60% drawdown.

But none of that changes what actually matters. The FDA approval was real. The product works. The plant in Durham is live. The dialysis submission is on track for 2026.

The only thing that matters now is proof. Real orders, real revenue, real adoption of SYMVESS. That’s what rewrites the chart. The rest (dilution, debate, doubt) are temporary side effects of being early. Once the data and dollars line up, price catches up fast. 

*Link: https://afterhour.com/YungEmber/8u7/dollarhuma-series-post-12-a-br*

### Post #214: 2025-11-02 14:23 — `$HUMA` `[Dd]`

**$HUMA Series Post 13: A Glimpse into 2028**

November 2028.

Three years ago, this stock was left for dead. Back in 2025, Humacyte was trading under $2! The FDA approval for SYMVESS had already come and gone, but all anyone could see were margin calls, insider optics, and two “poorly timed” capital raises. The talking heads called it “the classic biotech fade.” What they missed was that the story was just beginning. 

Fast-forward to now: $HUMA trades above $65 with a $9 billion valuation and gross margins that rival the med-tech elite. What was once a speculative biotech is now the industrial base of regenerative medicine.

SYMVESS, the acellular human vessel that started it all, is now standard of care in vascular trauma and DoD networks. The dialysis graft received approval in 2027 and has begun penetrating the $1.5 billion U.S. vascular-access market. But the real story is what comes next.

	•	Peripheral Artery Disease (PAD): Pivotal trial hit its endpoints mid-year with unmatched durability and zero infections. The PAD market exceeds $3 billion annually in the U.S. alone; Humacyte’s submission is targeted for 2029.

	•	Coronary Artery Bypass Grafting (CABG): First-in-human data showed >90% patency in small-diameter 3.5mm vessels. The CABG market tops 400,000 procedures a year, a potential $10 billion opportunity.

	•	Pediatric Heart Repair: Early implants under FDA Expanded Access grew with patients, eliminating the need for repeat surgeries. Investigational Device Exemption (IDE) filing expected 2029, allowing tests in humans before approved for commercial sale.

	•	Biovascular Pancreas: Large-animal studies underway; aims to create vascularized scaffolds for insulin-producing cells in Type 1 diabetes, a $25 billion global market if it works.

Meanwhile, the Durham LUNA200 plant hums at full capacity, producing >5,000 human vessels a year at ~70% gross margins. The same “dilution-funded” infrastructure from a few years ago, now prints cash. Turns out the only thing Humacyte needed was a little time.

### Post #215: 2025-11-03 18:47 — `$HUMA` `[Dd]`

**$HUMA Series Post 14: Analyst Snapshot**

Seven analysts are currently covering $HUMA. The spread says it all, a low of $3, a high of $25, and an average of $8.29. Targets are all over the place, but 6 of the 7 are rating it a Buy. The one Hold rating is from 2024 when $HUMA was trading around $5. Despite the near-consensus rating, the analysts are talking about two completely different companies.

At the low end of the price range are the analysts modeling Humacyte like a biotech. They’re focused on burn rate, trial timelines, and dilution risk. In their view, SYMVESS is still an experiment. Their spreadsheets assume slow adoption, modest revenue, and high fixed costs. The revenue line barely twitches until new indications like dialysis or PAD get approved, and EPS stays red through 2027. 

They’re building risk-adjusted cash flow models, discounting every future dollar because they still see Humacyte as pre-commercial. That math lands them near $3–$5/share. It’s the safe, conservative way to stay early without being wrong. But still a large upside from current prices. 

Then there’s the analysts that view $HUMA less as running a science project and more like the scaler they are becoming. These analysts already see Humacyte as a med-tech platform, not a clinical story. They’re modeling hospital reorder data, reimbursement traction, and margin expansion as Durham scales. In their view, the science is done, the question now is how fast hospitals standardize SYMVESS.

Their models have the company turning profitable next year. They have EPS climbing from $0.81 in 2026 to $2.22 in 2027 as volume and pricing power kick in. They’re using med-tech comps, other names that went from niche devices to billion-dollar platforms once adoption curves steepened. At a $25/share target, that’s a ~$4.7B valuation, 12–20× forward sales, and fully consistent with how the Street prices proven med-tech ramps.

Both sides are right, they’re just playing on different timelines. The biotech models end at proof-of-concept. The med-tech models start there.

Right now, the biotech view dominates, and given the slow trauma rollout, that’s fair. But when reorder data builds and hospital utilization starts compounding, the Street will have to scrap those biotech assumptions overnight. When that happens, the $25 target won’t look aggressive. It’ll look inevitable. 

*Link: https://afterhour.com/YungEmber/8HZ/dollarhuma-series-post-14-anal*

### Post #217: 2025-11-04 14:23 — `$HUMA` `[Dd]`

**$HUMA Series Post 15: Earnings Setup**

Analysts are expecting just over $900K in Q3 revenue for $HUMA, about 35–40 Symvess grafts at roughly $24K each. That’s a microscopic bar for a company scaling into national distribution.

CEO Laura Niklason has said that “the vast majority of first-year Symvess sales will occur in the back half of the year.” Through mid-2025, Humacyte booked roughly $250K in Symvess revenue, about 10–12 grafts at an ASP near $24K. That tiny base makes the setup obvious: even modest back-half traction across new civilian and military channels could multiply revenue several-fold against near-zero expectations.

As of early August, Humacyte had 82 civilian hospitals VAC-approved to purchase Symvess, up from just five in May. With the sales team ramping through Q3 and multi-hospital networks still moving through review, that footprint likely now exceeds 100 approved hospitals heading into earnings. 

Add to that the Defense Logistics Agency ECAT listing, which opened Symvess access to 35 military treatment facilities and ~160 VA hospitals, and the commercial reach has more than doubled since mid-year.

Hospitals don’t order immediately post-approval. Onboarding, contracting, and first-use planning take 30–90 days, but the pipeline is loaded. Even modest conversion beats expectations:

40 active sites × 3 implants = $2.9M
80 sites × 3 implants = $5.8M

On the expense side, I expect quarterly operating costs to remain near $30 million, mostly tied to R&D and commercial buildout. At this stage, EPS doesn’t matter as much given the large fixed cost relative to small sales volume. Even a big revenue beat will only minimally impact EPS. Once revenue begins to scale, fixed SG&A and manufacturing overhead absorb quickly, setting the stage for a much cleaner operating profile into 2026.

A revenue beat against a modest ramp assumption while maintaining a mostly fixed expense base, showing progress on VAC approvals. Thats what it will take for this earnings release to be a good one with solid price action. At sub-$1M revenue consensus, if we were to see $3M - $5M in revenue, which I think is very possible, I think share price goes $4+ in short order, setting up a major rally in 2026. 

*Link: https://afterhour.com/YungEmber/8Oi/dollarhuma-series-post-15-earn*

### Post #218: 2025-11-04 14:57 `[News]`

**Energy Vault expands into the Balkans**

$NRGV signed a Framework Supply Agreement with EU Green Energy, one of the leading renewable developers in the Balkans. The deal covers up to 1.8 GWh of battery storage deployments across Albania, Kosovo, Montenegro, and North Macedonia over the next four years using the company’s B-Vault battery system and VaultOS energy management platform.

The first project is a 100 MW / 400 MWh system in southern Albania and has already begun development. Phase 1 (50 MW / 200 MWh) is contracted and scheduled for commercial operation in Q3 2026, followed by Phase 2 in early 2027, pending final legislative approval.

B-Vault is Energy Vault’s modular, utility-scale battery storage platform built for rapid deployment and seamless integration with VaultOS, the company’s AI-driven software that optimizes asset performance, grid response, and revenue stacking.

This isn’t part of Asset Vault, it’s a straight technology and services deal, but it adds another layer of validation to Energy Vault’s platform as it scales globally. With this announcement, the company’s contracted and deployed B-Vault portfolio now tops 2 GWh, enough storage capacity to power roughly 200,000 homes for four hours.

The footprint is spreading fast and it’s starting to look like the software-first storage model Energy Vault’s been building toward is finally taking shape.

### Post #220: 2025-11-05 15:46 — `$HUMA, $TSLA` `[Dd]`

**$HUMA Series Post 16: The Bear Case**

To be fair, the bear case isn’t crazy. And if you’re going to understand this story, you have to see it through their lens.

The typical argument goes something like this:

* The company keeps raising money without results. 
* The CEO’s a visionary who talks about changing the world but hasn’t proven they can run a profitable business. 
* The product is impressive but niche, expensive, and unproven at scale. 
* The balance sheet’s thin, the dilution constant, and the skeptics aren’t wrong when they say execution risk outweighs promise. 
* Every quarter seemingly brings new delays, new explanations, new funding. 
* Insiders are selling and analysts are turning cautious. 

The company burns cash, overpromises, and looks perpetually six months away from validation that never quite comes. 

So as an investor, you have to sift through the noise to find what’s real. There are always two sides to the story. Much like the story you just read. It fits $HUMA, but those bullet points are the bear case for $TSLA in 2012. 

Now let’s see if the bears nail this one in 2025 like they did $TSLA in 2012. 🐉

### Post #231: 2025-12-03 21:39 — `$HUMA` `[Dd]`

**Still buying $HUMA**

Here’s your daily dose of copium.

I’m up to 500K shares of $HUMA and still sitting on my 12/19 calls because I can’t come up with any explanation for suspending an $80M open market sale agreement with Jefferies that isn’t fundamentally positive. An ATM is one of the most flexible ways a biotech can raise capital, and Laura has never shown any interest in pandering to short term investor sentiment. She has always prioritized long term clinical and operational execution. That is exactly why the voluntary removal of a major financing tool stands out. It implies either access to near term non dilutive funding or entry into a period where issuing new shares would not be appropriate. Some will chalk it up to removing dilution overhang, but nothing in Laura’s history suggests that investor optics drive her decisions.

I think there could be a structural layer at play. ATMs create unavoidable conflicts if a company is in sensitive strategic discussions. A bank that is selling shares cannot be anywhere near material non-public information, and no strategic counterparty wants a moving share count during evaluation. Given Laura’s track record of avoiding capital markets theatrics, ending the ATM is more likely a procedural step recommended by counsel or advisors connected to ongoing conversations. The move stabilizes the capital structure, reduces compliance risk, and lines up with what companies typically do before partnerships, government supported funding processes, or formal exploration of strategic alternatives including acquisition.

I am not advising anyone to chase 12/19 calls here. I am only holding the ones I already own. But I will keep accumulating shares. It feels awful to buy right now which is exactly why I’m doing it. It is never sexy until it is.

### Post #242: 2025-12-16 00:11 — `$HUMA`

**Is $HUMA prepping to be acquired?**

I’m not saying a deal is certain but an upcoming acquisition narrative fits the recent filings for $HUMA unusually well. Hear me out.

Back in January, there’s a flurry of Form 4s: stock options for the broader executive team, all struck above $4. That looks like a normal annual equity cycle, aligned with long-term upside.

But then in November two Form 4s appear, only for the CEO and CFO. The CEO receives options again, but at a much lower strike, which is normal given the price decline. The CFO, however, receives RSUs, not options, with vesting in May 2026 and May 2027.

That raises some natural questions:
	•	Why only these two executives?
	•	Why shift from options to RSUs for the CFO when options were previously standard?
	•	Why issue these grants outside the normal January cycle?
	•	And why does the first RSU tranche vest specifically in May, rather than the more typical annual cadence?

In any merger process, beyond the lawyers, the CEO and CFO are the two most critical executives. Losing either during a transaction can materially disrupt diligence, financing, disclosure, and integration. And when deals are announced, that’s often when recruiters circle hardest, exploiting uncertainty. And May 2026 feels like a good, conservative date for when a merger may be completed if currently underway. 

Viewed through that lens, these November grants can reasonably be interpreted as retention packages designed to keep key leadership in place through a sensitive window, rather than to reward long-term upside alone.

Layer on a few other recent developments. An 8-K clarified that Fresenius is no longer an insider, which looks like legal cleanup to remove ambiguity around reporting and affiliate status. Today, the company announced the retirement of its revenue-share agreement, funded by expensive but sale-compatible debt. Clean prepayment terms, clear change-of-control language, no poison pills. Revenue-share agreements are often a major sticking point for acquirers. Cleaning that up ahead of time not only makes sense, it may have been required.

Individually, none of this means much of anything. Taken together, though, it looks less like randomness and more like a company being made transaction-ready. Far from a guarantee but a narrative that I think fits the facts unusually well.  

*Link: https://afterhour.com/YungEmber/bcN3/is-dollarhuma-prepping-to-be-a*

### Post #251: 2025-12-31 21:35

**NFA but if most of your net worth is trapped in retirement accounts**

There are options for accessing the cash without a penalty before retirement age without any type of hardship. There is a lot involved with these options and I’m not giving you enough information in this post to make an informed decision. Just enough information for you to know these options exist. So do some research. Involve a CPA if planning to move forward. You don’t want to get on the wrong side of the IRS with either of these. For most people these do not make sense, so if you think it does, you better make sure and consult professionals. Information is power. There’s my disclaimer. 

ROBS (Rollover for Business Startups) lets you use retirement money to fund a new business. Your IRA rolls into a C-Corp 401(k), which buys stock in your new company. No taxes. No early-withdrawal penalty. There are companies that specialize in this and help ensure compliance. 

SEPP (Substantially Equal Periodic Payments) / 72(t). This is the opposite mindset. You’re converting an IRA into a scheduled income stream. No penalty but normal income taxes apply. The catch: once you start, you’re locked in for 5 years or until 59½ years of age, whichever is longer. You must take the calculated withdrawal each year for the exact dollar amount or penalties and interest will apply retroactively. You also can no longer add additional funds to this IRA (just open a separate account and you can fund that normally) but you can invest the existing funds as you see fit. There are three different methods for calculating how much must be taken out annually each with varying levels of complexity, risk, and dollar amounts. 

Again, this won’t make sense for most of you but I’m considering both so figured I would share as I was unaware of these until recently. 

*Link: https://afterhour.com/YungEmber/bdQU/nfa-but-if-most-of-your-net-wo*

### Post #264: 2026-01-07 01:27

**Somebody Knew Something**

Maybe. But most of the time, somebody just survived.

You aren’t in their tax bracket and can’t relate to putting that much capital in play. But degenerate gamblers exist at every level of net worth. If you’re wired that way, it’s the uncomfortable size of the bet and the outsized payoff that gets the dopamine flowing.

For every degen play that hits and gets attention on X, there are hundreds that went to zero. You’re seeing the one winner, not the graveyard behind it. Survivor bias in full effect.

It’s fun to conspiracize (this should be a real word). I do it too. Just understand the cost.

Once you start believing outcomes come from secrets instead of judgment, effort starts to feel pointless. Process feels naïve. Conviction erodes. The game feels rigged.

And when trust is gone, you don’t improve.
You either gamble harder… or you slowly stop playing. 

*Link: https://afterhour.com/YungEmber/bemh/somebody-knew-something*

### Post #295: 2026-01-13 22:40 — `$OSS`

**The OtterBox of Edge Compute**

When people think about AI hardware, they usually picture massive data centers run by cloud providers. That’s certainly part of it but a growing amount of AI can’t live in the cloud at all.

A lot of AI has to run where the data is created: inside vehicles, aircraft, drones, ships, and industrial machines. In those environments, latency matters, bandwidth is limited, connectivity isn’t guaranteed, and failure isn’t acceptable. That’s what people mean by edge compute. One Stop Systems ($OSS) builds the hardware for that world.

They design ruggedized GPU compute and high-speed storage that can survive heat, vibration, shock, dust, and tight power constraints. These systems process sensor data like video, radar, and lidar in real time, without relying on a remote data center. Normal servers don’t last here. These do.

What’s interesting is how the company has changed over the last couple of years.

$OSS used to be more spread out, selling a mix of rugged hardware, including lower-margin, more general OEM and distribution work. Over time, they made a conscious shift away from that and toward higher-value edge AI and defense programs. They trimmed non-core businesses, simplified the model, and focused on areas where performance actually matters and budgets are real.

They also shifted how they sell. Instead of just shipping boxes, $OSS increasingly works on customer-funded development, especially with defense and industrial partners. Customers pay them to design a solution for a specific platform. If that system gets deployed, $OSS often becomes the production supplier. Once you’re designed into a vehicle or platform, you tend to stay there for years.

As that mix has changed, margins have improved and the revenue has become more tied to long-cycle programs rather than one-off orders. The work is closer to mission-critical infrastructure and less tied to hype cycles.

This isn’t AI for training models or chasing clicks. It’s AI for seeing faster, deciding locally, and functioning when networks fail. If AI is becoming the brain of modern systems, $OSS builds the hardened body that lets it survive in the real world.

I picked up 10K shares. Not financial advice. 

$OSS 🦦📦⚙️

*Link: https://afterhour.com/YungEmber/be36/the-otterbox-of-edge-compute*

**Could $ONDS be prepping to spinoff Networks?**

Ondas Networks is already noticeably disconnected from the rest of the business. Having Networks spun off and announced on/around Investor Day would kind of make sense. A lot has happened in the last ~60 days:

• Networks debt maturity nudged just a couple weeks to Jan 15 (tomorrow)
• Assets/equity associated with Networks moved up to the parent
• “Holdings” dropped from the name signaling a unified operating company, not a grab bag.
• Everything not Networks has an Investor Day the day after that debt matures (Friday)
• HQ moving from where Networks made sense (Waltham, MA) to where autonomous systems make sense (West Palm Beach, FL)

Could be coincidence. Could be nothing. Perhaps Eric is content to minimize spend within Networks and just wait until the rails are ready for the upgrade. I’m positioned for Investor Day regardless.

### Post #304: 2026-01-18 01:46

**My only issue with Kinfo so far**

Here’s the scenario. I bought and sold shares of a ticker in 2025. In 2026 I bought that same ticker but Kinfo only captured the sell in 2025 and not the buy so now it thinks my 2026 purchase was closing a short position. Ticker is higher in 2026 than when I sold it so it’s showing as a $15K loss. It may correct itself once I sell the shares I bought in 2026. Idk but I doubt it. 

I am guessing the initial data feed to Kinfo only goes back so far so it makes sense based on the data available to them that they would classify as a short, but there should be a process to request errors to be corrected, which I don’t see. Zero response from the feedback submission. That’s the bigger annoyance. 

Total is actually a little over $41K of realized gains YTD. 

*Link: https://afterhour.com/YungEmber/bftY/my-only-issue-with-kinfo-so-fa*

### Post #339: 2026-02-13 22:40 — `$OSS` `[Dd]`

**$OSS Refreshed Corporate Deck**

Ever wonder how a fighter jet processes data in real time, or how an Army vehicle “sees” the battlefield using dozens of sensors at once? That takes serious computing power, the kind you’d normally only find in a massive data center. The problem is, you can’t strap a data center to a combat vehicle.

That’s where One Stop Systems comes in. They take the most powerful computing hardware on the market and shrink it down into rugged, battle-ready packages that can survive extreme heat, cold, vibration, and shock. Think of them as the company that brings supercomputer-level power to places where computers really don’t want to be.

Why should you care? The military is going through a massive upgrade cycle. Every branch wants AI, autonomous systems, and real-time sensor processing pushed out to the field, not sitting in a server room thousands of miles away. OSS builds exactly what they need. The global market for this type of rugged computing is estimated at $17B and growing fast as legacy systems simply can’t keep up with what AI demands.

The pipeline tells the story. OSS is sitting on over $1 BILLION in potential deals. Within that, they’ve identified roughly $450M in platform opportunities through 2028. These aren’t one-and-done sales. Once OSS wins a platform (like a specific aircraft or vehicle program), they become the locked-in supplier for years of production, upgrades, support, and tech refreshes. That’s the kind of recurring, predictable revenue that compounds over time.

Where’s the growth coming from? Four big lanes. 

First, defense vehicles: there’s a U.S. Army project on the table that could mean 5,000+ systems across multiple types of combat vehicles. 

Second, military aircraft and maritime: they’re already embedded on the Navy’s P-8 submarine-hunting aircraft and expanding into new programs. 

Third, commercial data centers: they just launched Ponto, the world’s first PCIe Gen 5 GPU expansion system built for high-density AI workloads. 

Fourth, international markets, which add roughly $1B to their addressable opportunity and are a major focus going forward.

What makes them hard to compete with? They’re one of NVIDIA’s select manufacturing partners and the only one at their level with a license to design around NVIDIA’s most advanced chip-linking technology. That’s a huge deal. 

They’ve also got security clearances to handle classified military work, which is a barrier most competitors can’t easily cross. And they’re already developing next-generation PCIe Gen 6 technology, staying ahead of the curve before competitors even catch up to Gen 5.

The leadership team is stacked with veterans from Lockheed Martin, Raytheon, and Honeywell who’ve spent decades winning big defense contracts. These aren’t startup guys figuring it out. They’ve done this before, and they’re actively signaling that M&A is next. With zero debt, a strong cash position, and $22.4M from a recent divestiture, they have the firepower to make bolt-on acquisitions that add new customers, technologies, and platforms to the business.

The roadmap ahead: management is targeting double-digit revenue growth and double-digit EBITDA margins over the next few years. Gross margins are already running in the mid-40s and should keep expanding as higher-value programs ramp. They expect to be EBITDA profitable for 2025 and the trajectory from here is about scaling, not turning things around.

Market cap is hovering around $200M. The pipeline is 5x the size of this company. Revenue is accelerating. Margins are expanding. Management is executing. 

And most people have never heard of them. 

Now that you’ve had a primer, go read the full presentation and let me know in the comments how many shares you plan to buy on Tuesday.

### Post #346: 2026-02-20 01:30 `[Dd]`

**Iran won’t be different**

Every major US military engagement of the last 60 years started the same way: limited scope, clear objectives, in and out.

Vietnam started with advisors. Iraq started with no-fly zones. Libya started with “protecting civilians.” Syria started with arming proxies. None of them stayed small. And none of them ended when they were supposed to.

We’re watching this unfold with Iran right now.

In June, the US bombed three Iranian nuclear sites using B-2 bombers and the largest conventional munitions in the US arsenal. Trump called the facilities “obliterated.”

Eight months later? The International Atomic Energy Agency says Iran could resume enrichment within months. Iran is rebuilding and fortifying its sites, conducting military drills in the Strait of Hormuz, and the US is deploying two carrier strike groups with a full military buildup expected to follow.

The “limited, targeted” strike didn’t work. And now the options are: accept that reality, or escalate. We know which one Washington picks. Every time.

How long could this drag on?

Afghanistan was supposed to be a quick strike on al-Qaeda. It lasted 20 years. Iraq was “Mission Accomplished” in 2003. Troops didn’t fully leave until 2011, went back in 2014, and are still there today. Vietnam escalated from 900 advisors to over 500,000 troops. Syria was never supposed to involve ground forces at all. That was 2014. We’re still there.

Iran checks every box for a prolonged engagement: 90 million people, mountainous terrain, a reconstituting missile program, proxy networks across four countries, and a regime that absorbs punishment rather than capitulates.

Now look at what the US did in Venezuela, and ask yourself why.

Operation Southern Spear was sold as a counternarcotics campaign. The US started striking drug boats in September. By December, it was seizing oil tankers and imposing a full blockade. On January 3rd, US forces captured Maduro and Trump announced the US would “run the country.” The Gerald Ford carrier group redeployed from the Caribbean to the Middle East on February 12th.

Venezuela has the largest proven oil reserves on the planet. If you’re about to confront the country that controls the Strait of Hormuz, where roughly 20% of the world’s oil flows, one of the biggest risk is what happens to energy markets. Iran’s parliament already voted to close the Strait during the June conflict. They’re running military drills there right now.

Securing Venezuelan oil before escalating with Iran isn’t a coincidence. It’s a hedge. If Iran disrupts Gulf oil flows, the US now has direct influence over an alternative supply source to stabilize global oil markets. The “drug war” was energy security positioning. The timeline makes that hard to argue against.

Let’s also consider that war has historically been an economic engine.

WWII pulled the US out of the Great Depression. GDP roughly doubled between 1939 and 1945. Korea and Vietnam coincided with strong economic expansion. After 9/11, defense spending surged from $300B to over $700B and created entire new industries. That money rippled through supply chains, logistics, tech development, and energy infrastructure across the broader economy.

Prolonged military engagement drives sustained government spending that functions as economic stimulus, creating winners across defense and adjacent sectors for years beyond what the market initially prices in.

The market is pricing in a diplomatic resolution or a short engagement.

History says that’s the wrong bet. Position accordingly. 

*Link: https://afterhour.com/YungEmber/bibt/iran-wont-be-different*

### Post #347: 2026-02-20 23:10 `[News]`

**Understanding Trump’s New Tariff Strategy**

The Supreme Court ruled 6-3 today that Trump’s IEEPA tariffs are illegal. Within hours, he signed a new executive order imposing a 10% global tariff under completely different legal authority. Here’s what happened and what comes next.

What got struck down

Every tariff imposed under the International Emergency Economic Powers Act. The Liberation Day reciprocal tariffs, the fentanyl tariffs on Canada, Mexico, and China, the country-specific rates as high as 125%. The court said IEEPA does not authorize tariffs. The word “tariff” never appears in the statute. No president had ever used it this way. Chief Justice Roberts wrote that Trump “asserts the independent power to impose tariffs on imports from any country, of any product, at any rate, for any amount of time,” but that the law “cannot bear such weight.”

What still stands and what each law requires

The Constitution gives Congress the sole authority to levy tariffs. But over time, Congress has delegated pieces of that power to the president through specific trade laws. Each comes with different criteria and different limits. The tariffs imposed under these authorities were never challenged and remain fully intact.

Section 232, Trade Expansion Act of 1962

The president can restrict imports if they threaten national security, but only after the Commerce Department conducts a formal investigation and makes that finding. There’s no cap on the rate and no time limit, but the national security justification has to hold up. This covers the current 50% tariffs on steel, aluminum, and copper, 25% on automobiles, and 100% on certain pharmaceuticals. A dozen more investigations are underway covering semiconductors, critical minerals, drones, robotics, and medical equipment.

Section 301, Trade Act of 1974

The president can impose tariffs to retaliate against unfair foreign trade practices like IP theft, forced technology transfer, or market access barriers. The U.S. Trade Representative must investigate first, a process that takes months. No cap on the rate. This underpins the existing China tariffs from his first term and the new investigations Trump announced today.

Section 122, Trade Act of 1974

The president can impose a temporary import surcharge to address “large and serious” balance-of-payments deficits. No investigation required. But it’s capped at 15% and limited to 150 days, after which Congress must extend it. The default is global application. The president can exempt countries but can’t target specific ones. This is what Trump invoked today for his 10% global tariff. It has never been used before. 

What comes next 

The tools are narrower now. The intent hasn’t changed. Section 122 replaces the baseline. Sections 232 and 301 remain in full force and are expanding. The 150-day clock on Section 122 expires in mid-July, and whether Congress extends it in the middle of midterm season is far from certain. Then there’s the money already collected. Up to $175 billion in IEEPA tariffs were paid by importers over the past year, and the Supreme Court said nothing about whether that gets refunded. That fight is likely heading to the lower courts next. 


*Link: https://afterhour.com/YungEmber/big8/understanding-trumps-new-tarif*

### Post #355: 2026-02-27 20:02 — `$ONDS`

**What’s going on with $ONDS today?**

Someone bought 49K puts at a $5 strike expiring in Jan 2028. $9M premium paid. Bearish right? 

Shares available to short went from 850K to 50K. Bearish right? 

Here’s what’s happening. 

A huge long holder (4.9M shares) is hedging and capping the downside risk. That’s the reason for the 49K puts bought. If this were really a bearish position, they wouldn’t buy 2 years out. 

The other side of that equation is the market maker that sold the puts. Now the market maker has to hedge by borrowing a fuck ton of shares to short. This makes them indifferent to the price movement and they can just harvest the premium *nearly* risk free. 

Still think it’s bearish?

### Post #360: 2026-03-06 01:46 — `$AAPL`

**The Characters of AfterHour**

Who am I missing? 

The Archaeologist
Digs up a micro cap nobody's heard of, drops a 3,000-word DD at midnight, and wakes up to one comment that says "positions?" Has read every 10-K since 2019.

The Vibe Trader
No charts. No filings. Buys based on energy. Sells based on energy. Somehow outperforms the Archaeologist. 

The Widower
Still holding a position from 2021 that's down 90%. Won't sell. Can't sell. Has rewritten the thesis three times to justify staying in.

The Commenter
First comment on every post. Always. "Great DD!" "Following this." "In for 500 shares." Adds nothing. Misses nothing. The app's heartbeat.

The Buzzkill
Posts bear cases on stocks people love and acts surprised when the comments turn hostile. "I'm just presenting the data." Sure. Enjoy your 47 notifications.

The Screenshot Artist
Green day? Screenshot. New all-time high? Screenshot. Portfolio up 11%? Believe it or not, screenshot. Down 30%? Phone goes dark for two weeks.

The Historian
Never posts before a move. Always shows up after. "Yeah, I saw that coming. If you read the 10-Q it was obvious." Has never once shared a take in real time. Undefeated in hindsight.

The Arsonist
Says something wildly wrong on purpose and walks away. "$AAPL is basically a penny stock." Then watches the comments burn from the other room. Has no positions. Just wants chaos.

The Furu
"Join my Discord for real-time alerts. Link in bio. First month free." Always has a promo code. Never has a track record. 

The Prince of Nigeria
New account. No post history. Profile pic is a stock photo of someone in a suit. Comments about a "guaranteed 10x opportunity" and a WhatsApp group. Reports say they're also a Saudi oil heir and a retired hedge fund manager. Busy person.

The Enforcer
Runs background checks on strangers for free. Reverse image searches profile pics. Tracks post history across platforms. The moment a scammer shows up, The Enforcer is already three comments deep with receipts. Unpaid, unasked, undefeated.

The Meme Lord
Doesn't do DD. Doesn't need to. Posts a rocket emoji and a gain screenshot that makes no mathematical sense. Comments "lol" under serious analysis. Has a better return than most. Will not elaborate.

The Philosopher
Doesn't post tickers. Posts things like "the market is a mirror" and "your risk tolerance is your autobiography." You're not sure if they're profound or just high. Might be both.

The Local Celebrity
10,000+ followers, which on this app makes them Mr. Beast. Has beef with another Local Celebrity over a semiconductor take from 8 months ago. Neither will let it go.

The Newsroom
Reposts headlines from CNBC with zero commentary. Just the link. Sometimes adds "thoughts?" Never shares their own. Somehow has 2,000 followers.

The Insider (Who Definitely Isn't)
"I can't say too much but... let's just say something big is coming." Posts this every 3 weeks about a different ticker. Has never been right. Will never stop.

The Therapist
Responds to every red day panic post with "zoom out" and "you only lose when you sell." Genuinely trying to help. Has talked at least one person off a ledge. The app doesn't deserve them.

The Old Head
Been investing since before apps existed. Mentions their "cost basis from 2008" like a war story. Their boring dividend portfolio has quietly outperformed everyone else.

The Gambler
0DTE options only. Calls it "investing." Their Robinhood account has been funded 14 times already this year. Massive following. Not because anyone wants the picks. They just can't look away.


*Link: https://afterhour.com/YungEmber/bjeU/the-characters-of-afterhour*

### Post #361: 2026-03-09 03:26

**Stop staring at red lines and read up on this fly brain thing so we can figure out how to make money off of it**

A startup called Eon Systems copied a fruit fly’s entire brain into a computer this week. Then they dropped it into a virtual fly body running real physics and flipped it on.

It started walking immediately. 

This wasn’t an AI trained on “fly data” or a model optimized to mimic a fly. This was the actual wiring of a dead fly’s brain, reconstructed from electron microscope images, and it moved on its own immediately. 139,255 neurons and 50 million connections fully mapped. 

The map took ten years. A project called FlyWire sliced a real fly brain into 7,000 sections, imaged every one, and used AI to trace the wiring. The AI wasn’t good enough on its own so they turned the proofreading into a game and recruited thousands of people to check the work. Once the wiring was mapped they used machine learning to predict the neurotransmitter each neuron uses (dopamine, glutamate, GABA, etc.). This allowed them to know which connections excite and which ones inhibit and to what degree. 

Eon says mouse brain is next with 70 million neurons. Then human brain with 86 billion. This feels like the start of a Black Mirror episode. 

https://x.com/alexwg/status/2030217301929132323?s=46

### Post #362: 2026-03-10 01:14

**He’s just buying time**

Today Trump told CBS the war is “very complete, pretty much.” This afternoon, the Pentagon posted “We have Only Just Begun to Fight.” Consistent messaging has been a big struggle during this “small excursion.” 

Trump doesn’t need the war to actually be over. He needs you to think it’s almost over long enough for oil to come back down and markets to stabilize. Crude nearly hit $120 a barrel this morning. Then Trump floated taking over the Strait of Hormuz in the CBS interview. Oil dropped 10% in two hours. Stocks closed green after bleeding all day. 

This is not a president reporting on the war. This is a president trading the war.

His play is straightforward: project confidence, hint at control of the Strait, dangle the end of hostilities just long enough for markets to price in a short conflict. If gas prices cool off and 401(k)s stop bleeding, public patience extends. And patience is the only resource that actually matters right now, because nothing on the ground supports a quick resolution.

Iran just swore in Mojtaba Khamenei as supreme leader. The IRGC pledged full loyalty. And I’m sure the new supreme leader is big mad that we killed his dad. 

Iran has launched over 500 ballistic missiles and nearly 2,000 drones since Feb 28 and analysts think the declining launch rate may reflect rationing for a longer war, not defeat. 

Trump knows the difference between destroying military hardware and ending a war. Every president who’s bombed the Middle East knows that difference. He’s not confused. He’s running the clock.

The gamble is that he can reopen Hormuz and calm the economic bleeding before the American public figures out this is a long haul. If he can, the politics work. If he can’t then the “pretty much complete” line becomes his “Mission Accomplished” moment. 

Defense. Energy. Gold.

### Post #369: 2026-03-23 23:53

**Unhinged Game Theory**

Disclaimer: This is a long post and I’m not providing any direct investment advice. If you want to be spoon-fed tickers, this ain’t the post for you. 

I’ve posted a few times that I think the Iranian war will drag out for a while. I also believe that Trump is in favor of regime change and this is his ultimate goal with Iran. Let’s analyze the situation with a game theory approach. Game theory isn’t prediction. It’s mapping what rational actors do when their incentives collide.

Let’s briefly set the stage.

The U.S. and Israel launched attacks on Iran on February 28th. Khamenei is dead. His son is the new Supreme Leader. Iran retaliated across the Gulf. The Strait of Hormuz is functionally closed. Brent is still around $100. Thirteen U.S. service members are dead, at least.

On Saturday Trump posted on Truth Social his 48 hour ultimatum. Iran responded that if its power plants were hit it would completely shut down the Strait and strike energy infrastructure across Israel and the Gulf.

Also over the weekend Iran fired two ballistic missiles at Diego Garcia, the joint U.S.-UK base in the Indian Ocean. Over 4,000 km from Iran. Every prior assessment had their range at 2,000 km. Iranian missiles can now reach London, Rome, Paris, Berlin.

The normal response to an adversary proving it can hit targets twice as far as anyone thought would be immediate escalation. Instead Trump announced a five-day pause citing “very good and productive conversations” with Tehran. Iran denied any talks occurred. Brent dropped 11% on the headline.

Hours after the pause Israel launched what reporters called “unprecedented” strikes on Tehran. The 82nd Airborne is on prepare-to-deploy orders. Two Marine Expeditionary Units are sailing toward the region.

Trump wants regime change. He told TIME he wants to help select Iran’s next leader. You cannot do that from the sky. Regime change requires a ground presence. A ground presence requires the American public to support sending troops into a country of 90 million people after Iraq and Afghanistan broke their appetite for exactly that. And it requires European involvement to provide coalition legitimacy and share the cost.

Right now Trump has neither.

The Players

Iran. Fractured regime. Their best remaining card is Hormuz. Diego Garcia was meant as deterrence. But signals get interpreted by the receiver, not the sender.

Israel. Needs the U.S. committed long enough to finish the job. Every escalation that deepens American involvement serves their interest. Not bound by the five-day pause.

The GCC. Getting hit with missiles they didn’t ask for. Want de-escalation but can’t push for it without alienating Washington. The involuntary board on which this game is played.

Europe. Was dragging its feet until Diego Garcia. Now every defense minister is staring at a range map with their capital inside the circle.

Russia. Wants America stuck. A Middle East quagmire drains resources from Ukraine.

China. Every day the Strait is closed costs billions. But a prolonged war accelerates de-dollarization. Time is on their side.

The American Public. Most important player. Least strategic. The Middle East is abstract. The homeland is not.

A Scenario

Let’s think about a scenario where the desired outcome is regime change and work backwards.

Regime change requires a ground presence. A ground presence effectively requires congressional and public support. Authorization and support require an event that makes the war feel existential to Americans personally. The Middle East doesn’t do that on its own, but the homeland does.

Should there be an Iranian-backed attack on American soil, even without casualties, troops on the ground become a rally cry. However, I’m not convinced that Iran has the ability to do this in its current fractured state. 

There are two players with both the incentive and the operational capacity to produce a domestic event falsely attributed to Iran. Those are the two players with the infrastructure to monitor and manage the very networks that would be blamed. A symbolic, non-casualty attack on the homeland may be the perfect approach for the player who wants the political effect without the investigative intensity.

Dead Americans create commissions. A close call creates a blank check.

Considering the recent “ceasefire” that Trump has announced, an attack on U.S. soil during this five-day period would be the ideal time to maximize effect. 

The Window

Trump created a five-day window with conditions only he can evaluate. Iran says no conditions exist. One player defines defection on their own terms.

A domestic attack during a “ceasefire” is the cleanest possible narrative to justify ground troops. They violated the peace. They hit us at home. They proved with Diego Garcia they have the range for this to be a global concern. Every piece of the story is pre-assembled.

Congress authorizes in days or at the least doesn’t stop Trump from acting. NATO Article 5 gets enacted pulling Europe into the conflict. The American public goes from war-weary to war-ready overnight.

And there’s a logistics angle. The Marines are still in transit. Ground operations require positioning that air campaigns don’t. A diplomatic pause is perfect cover.

The Equilibrium

Let’s map where every player lands with this scenario.
Trump gets consensus for ground forces to enact regime change. Israel gets permanent American commitment. An uneasy Europe after Diego Garcia gets pulled in through Article 5. Russia gets a quagmire that takes attention away from Ukraine. China gets accelerated decline of American hegemony through further indebtedness. Iran is too fractured to counter any narrative. The GCC can’t object.

Almost every player either benefits from or cannot prevent escalation. The players with the strongest incentive to prevent it, the American public and the GCC, have the least agency. Until the homeland gets hit. Then the American public’s agency activates in exactly the direction the other players need.

That’s a Nash equilibrium. Every player acting rationally and the convergence point is escalation.

Probably nothing though. And the story we are being told is certainly the truth. Just like with the WMDs that justified the Iraq invasion and the Gulf of Tonkin incident which justified escalation in Vietnam.


*Link: https://afterhour.com/YungEmber/bkrt/unhinged-game-theory*

### Post #375: 2026-03-29 16:42

**Why I’m not stocking up on canned goods and toilet paper here in the U.S.**

Everyone has an opinion on this war. Most of them are noise. Understanding global supply chain dependency is critical to understanding regional impacts. Making blanket statements without nuance is reckless analysis. So here’s the nuance.

A region-by-region breakdown of who survives a prolonged Middle East conflict and who doesn’t. No sentiment. Just inputs and geography. Risk rated 1-10. 1 is inconvenience. 10 is societal breakdown. This isn’t a short-term assessment of markets. Markets can at times act irrationally, but eventually the risk and impact will be appropriately priced. Your guess is as good as mine on when that happens. 

United States - Risk: 2/10
Net energy exporter. Largest oil producer on earth. Massive refining capacity. Strategic Petroleum Reserve as a buffer. Breadbasket agriculture with freshwater surplus. Two ocean buffers and the only military that projects force globally. Americans pay more at the pump and complain. That’s the ceiling.

Europe - Risk: 7/10
Catastrophic exposure. Still recovering from the Russia energy decoupling and now the Hormuz corridor is threatened. 90% natural gas import dependency. Fertilizer costs already elevated. Manufacturing base hollowed out. No credible independent military deterrent. Europe doesn’t have a plan. Europe has a committee.

China - Risk: 7/10
80%+ of crude imports transit the Strait of Hormuz or the Malacca Strait. Both are now strategic liabilities. SPR is smaller than advertised and draw logistics are untested. Food import dependency rising. Water tables in the north collapsing. Factory economy runs on cheap energy inputs that just got repriced. The CCP’s social contract is prosperity in exchange for compliance. Remove prosperity.

India - Risk: 8/10
85% crude import dependent. Hormuz is the lifeline. A billion people on subsidized fuel and fertilizer. Fiscal deficit already stretched. Food production is domestic but diesel-dependent. One bad monsoon plus an energy shock and you get inflation that destabilizes governments. Modi’s margin for error is effectively zero.

Southeast Asia - Risk: 6/10
Mixed bag but mostly exposed. Singapore and Vietnam are trade-dependent. Indonesia has some domestic energy but refining gaps. Philippines imports almost everything. Thailand and Malaysia have partial buffers. The whole region depends on Chinese demand for exports. If China contracts they contract. Domino geography.

Russia - Risk: 4/10 short term. 8/10 long term.
Paradoxically benefits in the short term. Higher oil prices. Alternative buyer for anyone cut off from Western supply chains. But the economy is a wartime shell. Demographics are terminal. Sanctions have hollowed out industrial capacity. Russia sells crude at a premium while importing washing machines through Kazakhstan. Short term cash. Long term irrelevance.

South America - Risk: 5/10
Brazil is the sleeper. Energy independent. Agricultural superpower. Freshwater. Arable land. Mineral wealth. If Brazil had institutions it would be the biggest winner of the next decade. It doesn’t. Argentina has Vaca Muerta but no capital markets credibility. Venezuela has reserves but no functioning state. Colombia and Chile are import-exposed. The continent has the resources. It lacks the governance.

GCC - Risk: 5/10
They produce the energy but they’re sitting on top of the conflict. Revenue spikes from higher oil prices but infrastructure is targetable. Desalination plants run the entire water supply, which is a clear risk. Economies are diversifying on paper but still structurally petrodollar dependent.

Israel - Risk: 6/10
Active combatant. Iron Dome and Arrow systems are proven but not infinite. Economy is resilient and tech-heavy but mobilization pulls labor from the productive base. Energy is a vulnerability. No domestic oil. Gas from Leviathan and Tamar helps but doesn’t cover wartime demand. U.S. backstop is the margin of survival. 

Africa - Risk: 9/10
The most vulnerable continent and nobody is talking about it. Massive fuel and fertilizer import dependency. Food insecurity already at crisis levels in some regions. No strategic reserves. No refining capacity. Currency weakness makes every import more expensive. A sustained energy shock doesn’t cause hardship here. It causes famine.

Australia - Risk: 3/10
Energy exporter. Food exporter. Water-stressed but manageable. Geographic isolation is a feature. The vulnerability is trade concentration with China. If Chinese demand craters Australian commodity revenue follows. But in a pure energy war scenario Australia keeps the lights on and feeds itself. Worst case is a recession. Not a crisis. 


I’ll end with saying that the beginning of the end for the American empire began long ago, but I don’t see it fully unraveling for decades. This war is a contributing factor but there are many factors at play here, some far, far more impactful than this war. In fact, the war may even slow the decline in the short-term. 


*Link: https://afterhour.com/YungEmber/bkS3/why-im-not-stocking-up-on-cann*

**The Ballad of JD the Traveler**

Once there was a traveler who went from kingdom to kingdom spreading goodwill and smiling always and giving the thumbs up.

In the first kingdom he sat across from the warrior king who had survived three years of fire and siege and asked him if he had said thank you. The warrior king left empty handed and said thank you on his way out the door.

In the second kingdom he visited the Holy Father and the Holy Father died.

In the third kingdom he embraced the great lord who had ruled for sixteen years and could not be defeated and had bent the very laws of the land to his will. The great lord was defeated.

In the fourth kingdom he talked for twenty-one hours and boarded his carriage and before the carriage had crossed the mountain the king had declared war on the sea itself.

And the traveler smiled and gave the thumbs up and looked toward the horizon where the next kingdom waited and thought to himself that it was going very well.

And in the next kingdom a minister looked out his window at the sky and felt a chill he could not explain.

The traveler was already on his way.

### Post #378: 2026-04-16 23:30

**Why I Don’t Think This War Ends Quickly**

Religion has driven more human conflict than any other force. Despite the rhetoric around nuclear weapons, oil, and American hegemony, I feel the unspoken motivation is end-times prophecy. 

What’s rare about this conflict is the alignment. End-times stories from influential Jewish, Christian, and Islamic sects are lining up on the same map and the same timeline. We’re in a feedback loop where each tradition’s actions fulfill the other traditions’ prophecies.

Who believes this, and do they run anything?

Jewish Messianic Zionism: believes the Jewish return to Israel, sovereignty over Jerusalem, and a rebuilt Temple are the stages of redemption. 15-20% of Israeli Jews are in this camp but they are overrepresented in Israel’s current coalition and the Temple Mount activist movement.

Christian Dispensationalism: reads the Bible as a literal timeline: Israel reborn (1948, done), Jerusalem reclaimed (1967, done), Third Temple rebuilt, a Russia-Iran coalition attacking Israel, a seven-year peace deal with the Antichrist, Rapture, tribulation, Second Coming. Roughly 25% of American adults. Shapes US foreign policy toward Israel.

Shia Twelver Mahdism: The Hidden Imam returns amid chaos. The Hidden Imam is a messianic figure believed to have gone into hiding in the 9th century who will return to establish global justice before the end of the world. Chaos is a precondition to the return. This is Iran’s official state ideology and the faith of roughly 90-95% of Iran’s people. The Supreme Leader governs as the Hidden Imam’s representative.

Three state-adjacent movements. One war. Same headlines read as prophecy fulfillment by all of them.

What do all three agree on?
	1.	Jerusalem is where it ends
	2.	The Temple Mount is the flashpoint
	3.	A massive war with Iran/Persia at the center pulls in global powers
	4.	Damascus gets destroyed
	5.	A false savior deceives many before the true one arrives
	6.	Maximum suffering precedes redemption

One more thing. Ask yourself why five red heifers were flown from a Texas ranch to Israel in 2022. Jewish law requires the ashes of a perfect red, unblemished, never-yoked cow to ritually purify the priesthood before any Temple service can resume. No red heifer, no Third Temple. Look up who funded it and why. That’s the feedback loop in miniature.

Religious wars end when one side decides the cost of fighting exceeds the cost of losing what they’re fighting for. When it’s eternity, that calculation doesn’t resolve.

You don’t have to believe any of it. You just have to notice that the people in control of the weapons do.

### Post #385: 2026-04-26 14:45

**The Foxhole Effect**

When you survive something frightening with other people, your judgment of them changes for months afterward, in ways you will not notice and cannot easily reverse.

The mechanism is hormonal. A real threat triggers a cortisol spike. When the threat passes, cortisol crashes and oxytocin floods in to repair the system. Oxytocin is the bonding hormone. It does not discriminate. Whoever is with you when it floods gets bonded to you. Your body cannot distinguish between surviving a predator with your tribe and surviving anything that made you think, even for a second, that you might not walk out of the room. The wiring does not care that the person in this shared experience was your adversary an hour ago.

The bond is most intense for about two weeks. It tapers over two to three months. By six months it is mostly gone.
Inside that window, you give the other person a level of trust they have not earned. You extend latitude you would normally reserve for someone who has spent ten years proving themselves. They get it instantly, merely because their face was in the room when yours was.

Which brings us to last night at the Washington Hilton.

A few thousand journalists spent an hour under tables believing they could have just died alongside the President, the Vice President, the Speaker, and most of the Cabinet. 

These are the journalists we rely on, imperfect as they are, to tell us what this administration is actually doing. Their word choice, their framing, their decisions about what to lead with and what to bury. All of it shapes what the country and the rest of the world thinks is happening and how to make sense of it. Going into Friday, this administration was historically unpopular, fighting a war the country is split on, and actively suing the same news organizations whose reporters were now ducking under tables next to them. 

Curious to see what the next two weeks of coverage looks like for this administration. Curious to see what gets pushed through while the window is open. 


*Link: https://afterhour.com/YungEmber/bm0x/the-foxhole-effect*

### Post #414: 2026-06-02 16:08 — `$HUBC`

**Still not a squeeze; not even close**

$HUBC opened down 17% today, tripping the Reg SHO circuit breaker.

Rule 201 fires on a 10% intraday drop from the prior close. Once triggered, the restriction holds for the rest of that day and all of the next, regardless of where the stock trades after.

While active, short sales can only execute above the best bid. Shorts can’t hit the bid to push price down.
